Gould’s Library

The books and journals John Gould cited as his sources throughout Birds of Great Britain (1862–1873), extracted from the synonymy citations at the head of each species account and deduplicated across all 5 volumes.
413 of 414 distinct works verified with full APA citations and working links so far (ordered by how often Gould cites them). 2 could not be identified despite extensive lookup work — the searching phase for those is now closed (Jan’s call, 5 Aug 2026), though new material could always reopen one. 23 of 414 also have a researched author-and-significance write-up so far — who the person was, and what this reference actually offered Gould’s own readers and offers a browser today — a slow-growing feature, not a completed one. Every citation also shows which of the site’s species plates it’s attached to, and, where Gould’s own text gives one, the old Latin name he cited it under.
Author & significance Some say Linnaeus sat alone in Uppsala and simply out-thought two thousand years of natural history -- and it's true the binomial system he set out in the Systema Naturae (1758, 10th edition, the version zoologists still treat as nomenclature's starting line) is his own invention. But don't forget how much of the raw material behind it he never personally saw: Linnaeus sent out seventeen students -- his "apostles" -- to Palestine, Siberia, Java, Japan, South America, and the Cape, betting that their specimens and notes would fill in a system he'd already sketched the shape of. Seven of the seventeen never made it home. The Systema Naturae that made Linnaeus famous was as much a filing cabinet built to receive a global harvest as it was a solitary act of genius -- and the edition Gould actually cites most often, the 12th (1766), was itself revised in the light of specimens those students risked their lives to send back.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Apostles of Linnaeus, Wikipedia: Carl Linnaeus
Verified Linnaeus, C. (1766). Systema naturae per regna tria naturae: Secundum classes, ordines, genera, species, cum characteribus, differentiis, synonymis, locis (12th ed., Vol. 1). Laurentius Salvius.

Author & significance A country pastor in the small Thuringian village of Renthendorf spent fifty years doing something no one had asked him to do: shooting, skinning, and cataloguing every bird he could get his hands on, until his own attic held roughly 15,000 specimens -- one of the largest private bird collections ever assembled by a single person. That pastor was Christian Ludwig Brehm, and his 1831 'Handbuch der Naturgeschichte aller Vögel Deutschlands,' cataloguing some 900 species, is the single work Gould leans on hardest in this Library after Linnaeus himself -- cited 123 times. Brehm's exacting eye for tiny plumage differences made him a superb describer of birds, but it also earned him a reputation, still debated by ornithologists today, as someone who split too many populations into separate species that later turned out to be the same bird in different plumages. His own collection nearly vanished from history: an 1835 sale to the Berlin Zoological Museum fell through, and after Brehm's death the specimens sat forgotten in that same attic for decades, until the German ornithologist Otto Kleinschmidt rediscovered them and persuaded Lord Rothschild to buy roughly 9,000 skins in 1897. They reached the Natural History Museum at Tring in 1932, and today live at Museum Koenig in Bonn. Brehm's son Alfred went on to write 'Brehms Tierleben,' one of the best-known animal-life encyclopedias ever published in German -- meaning the family name reached far more readers through the son's popular book than through the father's obsessive, unglamorous cataloguing work Gould actually needed.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Christian Ludwig Brehm, Wikipedia: Alfred Brehm, Haffer (2003), "Christian Ludwig Brehm (1787-1864) on species and subspecies of birds", Journal of Ornithology (title/abstract only, re: splitting reputation)

Author & significance What does a twenty-nine-year-old naturalist do the moment he decides to catalogue all of nature? If you are Linnaeus in 1746, you start at home. 'Fauna Suecica' -- published as the animal companion to that same year's plant catalogue, 'Flora Suecica' -- set out to list every mammal, bird, fish, insect and worm known to live in Sweden, ordered and named by the same system Linnaeus was busy building for the rest of the living world. It is, after the Systema Naturae itself, the single work Gould leans on hardest in this whole Library -- 104 citations -- and it is worth noticing what that means: a book about one relatively small northern kingdom out-cites nearly every other source in a Library otherwise stuffed with grand multi-continent expeditions. There is a real complication buried in those 104 citations, too. The first edition of 'Fauna Suecica' appeared in 1746, twelve years before the 1758 edition of the Systema Naturae that the International Commission on Zoological Nomenclature treats as the official starting line for every modern animal name -- so the names in that first edition, mostly clunky multi-word Latin descriptions rather than tidy two-word binomials, don't count under today's rules, even though Linnaeus wrote them himself. He fixed this in a heavily revised second edition in 1761, rewritten in the clean binomial form his own later work had made standard, and it is that cleaner 1761 text -- visible in the tidy 'Falco haliaetus' and 'Falco milvus' style names behind Gould's own citations -- that actually did the work. Linnaeus had spent years travelling Sweden gathering the raw material for books like this, including a gruelling six-month, 2,000-kilometre trek through Lapland in 1732 on foot and horseback; 'Fauna Suecica' is where that decade of home-country fieldwork finally became a permanent, citable catalogue -- proof that before Linnaeus tried to name the whole world, he made sure he could first name his own back yard properly.
Checked against: Uppsala University Library news: "After 265 years: Linnaeus' Fauna Suecica translated into English" (2026), Wikipedia: Linnaeus' expedition to Lapland, Wikipedia: Carl Linnaeus (Flora Suecica / Fauna Suecica publication context), this project's own citations_parsed_gb.json (spot-checked several 'Linn. Faun. Suec' entries directly to confirm they use clean binomial names, consistent with the 1761 second edition rather than the 1746 first)

Author & significance Some say "Gmelin" in an 18th-century natural-history citation is just one man's name -- but don't forget the Gmelins were a whole scientific dynasty, and Gould's own citation list actually needs to keep at least two of them straight. The one behind "Gmel. edit. Linn. Syst. Nat" is Johann Friedrich Gmelin, who in 1788-93 took on the job of editing the posthumous 13th edition of Linnaeus's Systema Naturae -- swelling it with hundreds of newly described species, including birds John Latham had already described in English but never given a proper Latin name. He's a different man entirely from Samuel Gottlieb Gmelin (his cousin, once removed by a generation, who died young exploring the Caspian for the Russian Academy) and from Johann Georg Gmelin (an earlier-generation relative who surveyed Siberia). Three Gmelins, three separate expeditions and books, one surname doing a lot of unlabelled work across Gould's synonymies.
Checked against: HandWiki: Johann Friedrich Gmelin, University of Nottingham 'Making Science Public' blog: The Gmelin family

Author & significance Open a page of Gould's 'Birds of Europe' at random often enough and you will eventually hit a citation that reads simply 'Boie, Isis' -- 70 times across this Library, more than Brisson, more than Pallas. Isis was not a person. It was a journal, founded in 1816 by the philosopher-naturalist Lorenz Oken, and for its first fifteen years it was less a scientific periodical than a running argument with the German governments of the day: Oken used its pages to needle censorship laws and literary privilege, dragging in essays on medicine, technology, art and politics alongside the natural history. It cost him his professorship at Jena in 1819, hounded out under pressure from the post-Napoleonic Holy Alliance, and it earned Isis a description from Goethe -- in a private diary entry, not a compliment -- as a kind of Hydra, a many-headed thing that kept growing back no matter how the authorities tried to cut it down. Buried inside that Hydra, entirely unbothered by the censors circling its editor, a Holstein lawyer named Friedrich Boie used Isis's pages across the 1820s and 1830s to do quiet, careful taxonomic work: renaming and re-sorting European birds into tighter, better-defined genera. Athene, the genus that still holds the Little Owl today, is his; so is Cerchneis, once used for the kestrels; so is the swift genus Micropus. This project's own citation data confirms the pattern directly -- 'Athene noctua, Boie, Isis, 1826' and 'Cerchneis vespertinus, Boie, Isis, 1828' sit right there among the 70, unmistakably genus-level housekeeping rather than species description, spread across a working span from 1822 to 1844. Boie had a naturalist brother, too: Heinrich Boie, who died young in Java after describing dozens of reptiles and amphibians with Friedrich from opposite sides of the world, letters crossing the same ocean the specimens did. None of that family drama or journal politics shows up in a citation as terse as 'Boie, Isis, 1826, p. 315' -- but the paper trail is real, and it means that every time Gould reached for a Boie citation, he was pulling a small, orderly correction out of one of the most disorderly, most-watched periodicals in German science.

Author & significance Here is the irony sitting quietly inside 66 of Gould's citations: Mathurin Jacques Brisson refused, on principle, to use the two-word naming system -- one word for genus, one for species -- that Linnaeus was busy popularising in the very same decade. Brisson had trained for the priesthood before abandoning it in 1747 to curate the private natural-history cabinet of the great French scientist Réaumur at his Vendée estate, and when Réaumur died in 1757 he inherited both the collection and the unfinished project of cataloguing it properly. The result, published in 1760, was the six-volume 'Ornithologie' -- and Brisson filled it with long, multi-word Latin descriptive phrases rather than Linnaeus's tidy binomials, because he considered the two-word shorthand too imprecise for serious science. History has judged the two halves of that decision very differently. The descriptive species-phrases are gone, absorbed and forgotten. But the genus names Brisson coined along the way to organise his descriptions -- Gallinago for the snipes, Fratercula for the puffins, Grus for the cranes, Carduelis for the goldfinches, Mellisuga for the hummingbirds, dozens more -- turned out to be exactly the kind of stable, well-defined units zoology needed, and the International Commission on Zoological Nomenclature still recognises many of them today, unchanged. Linnaeus himself leaned on Brisson's fieldwork without much ceremony: the 1766 twelfth edition of the Systema Naturae folded in 386 bird species new to that edition, and Brisson's descriptions supplied 240 of them outright. So when Gould writes 'Briss. Orn.' after a bird's name, he is citing a man who out-argued Linnaeus's naming system and lost -- while quietly winning the much longer argument about what to call the genus in the first place.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Mathurin Jacques Brisson, web search: Mathurin Jacques Brisson naturalist biography 1723 1806 Ornithologie Réaumur

Author & significance Thirty-one years old when the breakdown hit that ended his research career for good. Before that, William Elford Leach packed in more taxonomic work than most naturalists manage in a full lifetime: appointed Assistant Keeper of Natural History at the British Museum in 1813 at twenty-three, he found the zoological collections in the kind of disorder that comes from decades of neglect, and spent the next eight years methodically fixing it -- publishing over 130 papers, creating more than 380 new genera, and dragging British zoology out of Linnaeus's increasingly strained artificial system and into the 'natural method' Cuvier and Latreille were developing on the Continent. His 1816 'Systematic Catalogue of the Indigenous Mammalia and Birds in the British Museum' -- the work Gould cites 59 times across this Library, more than Bechstein, more than Latham -- was one product of that campaign: a working inventory of Britain's own birds and mammals, rebuilt on the classification principles Leach was busy importing. He became, in his own time, the country's leading authority on crustaceans, and 137 species across multiple kingdoms would eventually carry some form of his name. Then in 1821 the pace caught up with him -- overwork triggered a breakdown severe enough to end his research outright, he resigned in March 1822 and left for Italy with his sister to recover, and he never returned to active science. He died of cholera near Tortona in 1836, forty-six years old, ten years into a retirement he spent corresponding with colleagues rather than publishing. One of those 137 namesake species is closer to home for this project than it first appears: Dacelo leachii, Leach's Kingfisher, the very bird whose account this project's own Birds of Australia gallery spent several passes chasing down after it turned out to be hiding, unlabelled, inside its neighbour's entry on the page. Leach never saw Australia and never described that bird himself -- the name honours him, given by others, the same way his crustacean work still quietly underpins a classification system most working naturalists today have never traced back to its source.

Mus., p. 9'), this project's own Birds of Australia Volume 2 project notes (Dacelo Leachii/Leach's Kingfisher recovery, confirmed as the same Leach namesake independently, not assumed from the name alone)

Author & significance Some say Peter Simon Pallas single-handedly braved the frozen wilderness of Siberia for Catherine the Great -- and it's true he endured six brutal years of it, returning with a catalogue no one else could match. But he wasn't sent out alone. Catherine's Academy of Sciences launched several "Great Academic Expeditions" at once in 1768, each naturalist handed a different slice of an empire too vast for any one man to cover: while Pallas pushed east through the Volga, the Urals, and the Altai, his contemporary Johann Anton Güldenstädt was doing the same work in the Caucasus, and others covered still other regions. Pallas became the most famous name to come home -- but he was really the star player on a whole squadron Catherine had sent out simultaneously, all racing to map the same unimaginably large blank space on the map.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Johann Anton Güldenstädt, general search on Catherine the Great's 1768 Academic Expeditions

Author & significance In 1832 a subscription went round among naturalists in London to help James Francis Stephens cover his legal bills -- he had just lost a copyright lawsuit against a fellow entomologist, James Rennie, and colleagues who valued his work stepped in. It was a fitting moment of exposure for a man who had spent his whole career living two lives at once: by day a clerk at the Admiralty office in Somerset House, a post his uncle Admiral Stephens secured for him in 1807 and that he held for thirty-eight years; by every other hour, one of Britain's most productive naturalists, working almost entirely off the books. He built his enormous insect collection with a pocket lens and a killing bottle rather than pins, an unconventional method that let him examine specimens most collectors of the day would have skipped, and it added up: roughly 2,800 British insect species described across the eleven volumes and supplement of his "Illustrations of British Entomology" (1827-1846), still a reference point for British entomology today. That same reputation for careful, high-volume taxonomic work is what put him behind the 56 citations in this Library reading "Steph. Cont. of Shaw's Gen. Zool" -- the continuation of George Shaw's "General Zoology, or Systematic Natural History," a 16-volume survey Shaw had reached only the eighth volume of before his death in 1813. Stephens picked it up and carried it through the final six volumes (1815-1825), covering most of the class Aves -- exactly the bird-by-bird groundwork Gould draws on here, citing real page and volume references like "Percnopterus cegyptiacus, Steph. Cont. of Shaw's Gen. Zool., vol. xiii. p. 7" (the Egyptian Vulture, its epithet garbled by OCR). The double life crossed paths with this Library's own previous entry, too: in 1818 Stephens took leave from the Admiralty to help William Elford Leach -- three years before Leach's own breakdown -- arrange the British Museum's insect collection, the same institution where Stephens would end up working unpaid for the rest of his life after his 1845 retirement. He married Sarah Roberts in 1822; their children died young, and Sarah outlived him. He died in December 1852, sixty years old, having spent his final years cataloguing a museum's specimens for no pay at all -- the government job long behind him, the naturalist's work the only one that had ever really counted.

Author & significance Some say Johann Jakob Kaup was in the grip of his own eccentric obsession, insisting nature organized itself into mystical circles of five. In fact the idea wasn't his at all -- it was invented in 1819 by a young Scottish naturalist, William Sharp MacLeay, working out of Sydney, and it was two British naturalists already elsewhere on this very citation list, Nicholas Vigors and William Swainson, who turned it into a full-blown London fashion, arguing it out in journals and drawing rooms for a decade. Kaup was one of the few Continental scientists to take the very British theory seriously enough to apply it rigorously -- to birds of prey, with real anatomical care. So the "circles of five" wasn't a lone German fixation; it was an international fad that crossed the Channel from a colonial magistrate's insect cabinet to a Darmstadt museum curator's raptors, with Vigors and Swainson as the go-betweens.
Checked against: Wikipedia: William Sharp Macleay, Wikipedia: Quinarian system

Author & significance Two names arguably belong on the title page of John Latham's "Index Ornithologicus" (1790) -- the 47-citation work behind the "Lath. Ind. Orn" entries in this Library. Only one appears. Latham himself lived to 96 (1740-1837), an exceptional span even by the standards of a profession full of long-lived naturalists, and packed a second career into the back half of it: he trained and worked as a physician in Dartford and then Romsey, retiring from medicine only in 1796 at age 56 to devote himself full-time to birds. By then he had already published his foundational "A General Synopsis of Birds" (1781-1785), and as ship after ship returned from the new penal colony at Botany Bay loaded with skins nobody in Europe had names for, Latham became the naturalist those specimens landed on -- he is credited with first describing the emu, the sulphur-crested cockatoo, and the wedge-tailed eagle, among others, earning him the informal title "grandfather of Australian ornithology." But the illustrations that turned his descriptions into publishable, citable science -- the plates that let a reader tell one new species from another -- were largely the work of his daughter Ann, who began drawing and painting birds and butterflies for him in her teens, contributing artwork to an account of Governor Phillip's Botany Bay voyage around the same years "Index Ornithologicus" was taking shape. In 1795 Ann married a surgeon, and, as was expected of a married woman of her class, stopped working as an illustrator entirely -- decades before her father stopped naming birds. Latham himself didn't escape a parallel erasure: because the German naturalist Johann Friedrich Gmelin's rival edition of Linnaeus's "Systema Naturae" beat "Index Ornithologicus" into print, priority under the rules of nomenclature went to Gmelin's names rather than Latham's for many of the very species Latham had described first. Gould's citations here -- specimens like "Sylvia ficedula, Lath. Ind. Orn., vol. ii. p. 517" -- are a reminder of work whose credit slipped away in two directions at once: forward, to a rival press; and sideways, to a daughter whose name never made it onto the page to begin with.

Author & significance You have probably never heard of Johann Matthäus Bechstein. You have almost certainly heard of the boy he took in. Bechstein was born in Waltershausen, in Thuringia, on 11 July 1757, and spent four years studying theology at the University of Jena before deciding he would rather be outdoors than in a pulpit or a classroom -- he tried teaching briefly and abandoned it for hunting, forestry, and natural history instead. In 1795 he founded a forestry school in his home town; his reputation was strong enough that in 1800 the Duke of Saxe-Meiningen put him in charge of a new one at Dreissigacker, near Meiningen, where he stayed for the rest of his career. Alongside the forestry work he wrote what became his signature achievement, a four-volume "Gemeinnützige Naturgeschichte Deutschlands nach allen drey Reichen" ("Useful Natural History of Germany, Across All Three Kingdoms") published 1789-1795 with a second, expanded edition in 1801-1809 -- the source behind the 47 citations in this Library reading "Bechst. Naturg. Deutschl," real entries like "Accentor alpinus, Bechst. Naturg. Deutschl., tom. iii. p. 700" and "Sylvia palustris, Bechst. Naturg. Deutschl., tom. iii. p. 639, tab. 26" drawn from the work's bird volume. He also wrote an early, influential guide to keeping cage birds, and was ahead of his time as a conservationist, arguing in print for the protection of animals -- bats among them -- that his contemporaries mostly regarded as vermin; a species of bat still carries his name today. Bechstein and his wife had no surviving children of their own, but in 1810 he adopted his nine-year-old nephew Ludwig, whose first years had been hard: born in Weimar, the illegitimate son of a Frenchman who vanished before the boy was born, Ludwig had grown up poor until his uncle brought him into a stable home and gave him an education. Ludwig went on to become a librarian, a historian, and above all a writer -- and in 1845 he published a "Deutsches Märchenbuch" ("German Fairy-Tale Book") that, on its first appearance, actually outsold the fairy-tale collection the Brothers Grimm had published three decades earlier. Two Bechsteins, one a working naturalist whose citations quietly hold up a page of Gould's "Birds of Great Britain," the other a storyteller whose versions of German folk tales were, for a time, the ones more German children actually grew up on.

Author & significance You have almost certainly heard of one Bonaparte who tried to redraw the map of Europe. This is the other one, who tried something quieter: drawing a map of where its birds actually lived. Charles Lucien Bonaparte (1803-1857), 2nd Prince of Canino and Musignano, was Napoleon's nephew -- son of Napoleon's brother Lucien -- and spent his twenties not in politics but in Philadelphia, having married his cousin Zenaide and moved into her father Joseph Bonaparte's household at Point Breeze, New Jersey. There he took up the unfinished work of the recently-dead Alexander Wilson, producing a revised, expanded American Ornithology (1825-1833), and in 1824 tried to get the still-unknown John James Audubon admitted to Philadelphia's Academy of Natural Sciences over the objections of the older, jealous George Ord. "Bonap. Geog. and Comp. List of Birds of Eur. and N. Amer" -- the 34-citation work behind these entries -- grew directly out of that transatlantic double life: a 92-page 1838 catalogue that set European and North American species side by side, line by line, so a reader could see at a glance which birds were genuinely shared between the two continents and which only looked alike. In his own preface Bonaparte called this "Ornithological Geography," a field he thought zoologists had neglected compared to what botanists had already done for plants -- and he leaned on Gould's and Audubon's illustrated works as the standard against which each region's species were checked, meaning Gould wasn't only a source he cited but also one of the two authorities he was citing back. One copy of that 1838 list, now digitized by the Darwin Online project under the shelfmark "CUL-DAR.LIB.54," carries the mark of Charles Darwin's own working library at Down House -- a book two future subjects of this same Library, Gould and Darwin, would go on to depend on for the very finch and mockingbird classifications that made them famous a year later. Bonaparte didn't stay a museum man for long, either: back in Europe by the 1840s, he threw himself into the failed Roman Republic of 1848-49, answering the revolutionary assembly's roll call with "Viva la Repubblica!", helping defend Rome against 40,000 French troops sent, with a bitter irony he cannot have missed, by his own cousin Louis-Napoleon -- and years later naming a bird-of-paradise Cicinnurus respublica in the republic's memory. He ended his life back in Paris, directing the Jardin des Plantes from 1854 until his death in 1857, credited by the time he died with well over 500 described species and 165 genera to his name.

Author & significance Picture a teenager in Amsterdam sometime in the 1790s, working through drawer after drawer of dead birds that are not, strictly speaking, his: they belong to his father, Jacob Temminck, treasurer of the Dutch East India Company, who built an enormous natural history collection by trading on the VOC's global shipping network without ever leaving the Netherlands himself. That teenager was Coenraad Jacob Temminck (1778-1858), and the collection he grew up cataloguing -- fed partly by specimens sent home by the explorer-engraver Francois Levaillant, a family friend who became the young man's informal mentor -- eventually became one of the founding collections of a national museum, with Temminck himself installed as its first director: head of the newly created Rijksmuseum van Natuurlijke Historie in Leiden from 1820, the institution now known as Naturalis, a post he held until his death in 1858. "Temm. Man. d'Orn" -- cited 28 times behind these entries -- is his own major work, the Manuel d'Ornithologie, first published in 1815 as a single volume covering the birds of Europe and then expanded into a much larger second edition across four volumes issued between roughly 1820 and 1840; several of the raw citations checked for this entry point specifically to that later, bigger edition ("2nd edit. tom. i.") rather than the slim 1815 original. For decades it was simply the standard reference for identifying a European bird -- the book a working ornithologist like Gould reached for as a matter of course. Temminck was not always right, and one case says a lot about the risk of writing the standard reference. In 1807 he described a genuinely tailless variety of junglefowl from Ceylon, naming it Gallus ecaudatus. Sixty years later, Charles Darwin, researching the origins of domestic fowl for The Variation of Animals and Plants under Domestication (1868), concluded that no such tailless bird existed and dismissed Temminck's account outright. Darwin was wrong: a tailless variant of the Sri Lankan junglefowl (Gallus lafayetii) is a real, if rare, natural mutation, and ornithologists re-examining the historical record in 2017 vindicated Temminck's original 1807 observation -- a correction arriving a century and a half after he had died convinced of what he had actually seen. It isn't only the disputed bird that carries a trace of him. A small, unremarkable European wader, Temminck's Stint, was named in his honor in 1812 by the naturalist Johann Leisler, while Temminck was still only in his early thirties, decades before the museum directorship that would define his later career. And near the end of his life, working from Leiden, he contributed the mammal volumes to the multi-part Fauna Japonica, drawing on specimens gathered by Philipp Franz von Siebold in a Japan almost entirely closed to Europeans at the time -- a country Temminck himself, like Gould with Australia, never once visited.

Author & significance At fifty years old, with his family's Caribbean trading fortune wiped out by revolution and, by his own account, two daughters newly dead of yellow fever on the voyage home, Louis Jean Pierre Vieillot had nothing left of his old life to go back to -- so he became an ornithologist instead. Born on 10 May 1748 in Yvetot, Normandy, he had spent roughly twenty years managing his family's cloth-trading interests in Saint-Domingue (Haiti), long enough to develop a serious private interest in tropical birds, before the Haitian Revolution forced him to flee to the United States around 1792. He spent about six years there, travelling the eastern seaboard from New York down to Florida and the Gulf, and did something few contemporaries bothered with: he kept live birds and watched them molt through the seasons, rather than working only from dead skins, so that he could describe plumages most European cabinet naturalists never saw. He sailed back to France in 1798 older, poorer, and by several accounts grieving, and spent the rest of his life -- accepting only a modest clerical post to get by -- turning that field experience into print. His Histoire Naturelle des Oiseaux de l'Amerique Septentrionale (1807-1808) drew directly on the American years, and his Analyse d'une Nouvelle Ornithologie Elementaire (1816) proposed a bird classification scheme that put him on a collision course with Coenraad Temminck -- another naturalist this library cites, at nearly the same shelf as this one. The 23-citation work behind this entry, "Vieill. Nouv. Diet. d'Hist. Nat" -- his ornithology contributions to the Nouveau Dictionnaire d'Histoire Naturelle, a sprawling collective encyclopedia issued in dozens of volumes between roughly 1816 and 1819 by an anonymous "society of naturalists and agriculturalists" -- is scattered across that set from as early as tome iii to as late as tome xxxiv, exactly the kind of far-flung, multi-volume citation pattern that makes a single author's name easy to lose track of. Vieillot never secured the state patronage his output arguably earned him, and died in poverty near Rouen in his early eighties -- sources disagree on whether it was 1830 or 1831 -- having described hundreds of species and founded genus after genus that ornithology still uses, cited by a much younger Gould working from entries in a dictionary whose actual authors' names rarely appeared on its own title pages.

Author & significance Bernhard Meyer (1767-1836) was, by day, exactly the kind of respectable provincial figure you would not expect to find behind a joke: a physician trained at Marburg, court doctor to the widowed Landgravine of Hesse-Kassel by 1793, and by 1796 the owner of Offenbach's Schwanen-Apotheke, the town's oldest pharmacy, founded in 1718. He and his wife Maria Eckhard, married that same year, went on to raise thirteen children there. And yet the same Dr. Meyer who dispensed medicine from behind the counter of the Swan Pharmacy is also remembered for lacing the sweets at an Offenbach masquerade ball around 1800 with a laxative, a prank respectable enough to survive in the local record two centuries later. "Meyer, Taschenb. Deutschl. Vg" -- the 23-citation work behind these entries, his Taschenbuch der deutschen Vogelkunde ("Pocketbook of German Ornithology"), produced with the illustrator Johann Wolf between 1810 and 1822 -- came out of the same man's quieter, more patient side: a private bird collection that eventually held 144 European species together with their nests, assembled and described alongside an earlier, larger joint work with Wolf, the Naturgeschichte der Vogel Deutschlands (1805-1821, issued in thirty parts). The collection was good enough that when Goethe passed through Offenbach in October 1814 and again in August 1815, he went to see it, later writing that "the collection of stuffed birds of Herr Hofrat Meyer deserves all attention." Meyer co-founded the Senckenberg Natural History Society in Frankfurt and, in 1819, gave his birds to its museum outright, forming the founding core of an ornithological collection that outlived him by well over a century. He died in Offenbach on the first day of 1836, sixty-eight years old, still remembered locally as much for the pharmacy and the prank as for the pocketbook of birds a much younger Gould would go on to cite twenty-three times, working from a small window in London, without ever knowing about the laxative candy.

Author & significance What do you do when a lighthouse engineer hands you a live Great Auk, tethered by a length of cord so it cannot dive out of sight? In 1821, touring Hebridean lighthouse works with the civil engineer Robert Stevenson, that is exactly the position the Scottish minister-naturalist John Fleming (1785-1857) found himself in, on the island of Scalpay off Harris. The bird had been caught off St Kilda by a local tacksman and passed on to Stevenson; kept alive on fresh fish and allowed to swim on its cord, it burst free while being washed near the island of Pladda, off Arran, and was never seen again. Fleming, who had already noted the black summer throat give way to a paler winter one, later wrote up the encounter as the only description any British naturalist ever gave of a living Great Auk -- not, despite what one modern summary of his life claims, the last British record of the species itself. That grimmer distinction belongs to the bird three men from St Kilda clubbed to death on the sea stack Stac an Armin in 1840, having decided it was a storm-conjuring witch. Fleming's working life ran on a similar collision of two callings. Ordained a Church of Scotland minister in 1808 -- first at Bressay in Shetland, then Flisk in Fife from 1810, Clackmannan from 1832 -- he was, by 1815, already reckoned the leading zoologist in Scotland, and went on to chairs of natural philosophy at King's College, Aberdeen (1834) and, after leaving the established Church for the Free Church in the 1843 Disruption, natural science at the new Free Church College in Edinburgh (1845). His two-volume Philosophy of Zoology (1822), five of its plates drawn by his wife Melville Christie Fleming under the initials "M.F." and engraved by William H. Lizars, made its way onto the Beagle's own bookshelf as one of the works Charles Darwin carried around the world. It was his next book, A History of British Animals (1828) -- the 21-citation source behind these entries, covering warblers and pipits, jays and nutcrackers, a scoter and a diver, page by page from p. 54 to p. 130 -- that put him on a collision course with the geologist William Buckland: Fleming argued in print, against Buckland and Cuvier both, that Britain's fossil beds and bone caves need not be read as relics of a single biblical deluge, and could just as easily reflect ordinary long-term climate change -- a position closer to the uniformitarian geology soon to come than to the catastrophism still dominant in the 1820s. He died at Sea Grove House, Leith, in November 1857, seventy-two years old, having spent nearly fifty years arguing, from the pulpit and the professor's chair alike, that watching nature closely and taking scripture seriously were not actually in conflict.

Author & significance Two students in their twenties, neither yet committed to a fixed career, spent parts of several summers in the 1830s tramping the Carpathians and the Alps together, netting and skinning whatever vertebrate life they could catch. One of them, Alexander von Keyserling (1815-1891), was a Baltic German count studying law at Berlin who kept drifting toward geology; the other, Johann Heinrich Blasius (1809-1870), was the son of a small farming family from near Cologne who had worked his way up from a teacher-training seminary to a place at Berlin University purely on the strength of self-directed study. Their fieldwork produced a first joint paper in 1837 and then, in 1840, a full book: Die Wirbelthiere Europas ("The Vertebrates of Europe"), published in Braunschweig -- the 21-citation source behind these entries in Gould's own Library, running page 32 to page 84 across warblers, pipits, larks, a cuckoo, and a shelduck. The two men's paths then split hard. Blasius stayed in Braunschweig, becoming professor of natural history at the Collegium Carolinum in 1836 and, from 1859, director of the city's new Natural History Museum (adding the Herzog Anton Ulrich Museum to his charge in 1866); his 1857 Fauna der Wirbelthiere Deutschlands extended the earlier joint project to Germany alone, and both of his sons, Rudolf and Wilhelm, grew up to become ornithologists in their own right. Keyserling, meanwhile, finished a Berlin doctorate in 1842, joined the Scottish geologist Roderick Murchison's 1841 survey of European Russia and the Urals alongside the French palaeontologist Edouard de Verneuil, and co-authored the resulting 1845 volume, The Geology of Russia and the Ural Mountains. He is remembered today at least as much for a much stranger claim: in 1853, writing in the Bulletin de la Societe Geologique, he proposed that new species might arise the way epidemic diseases seemed to, through "circumambient molecules of a particular nature" chemically altering the germs of existing forms. Charles Darwin thought the idea odd enough, and serious enough, to quote it by name in the Historical Sketch he added to the third edition of On the Origin of Species in 1861 -- one of the very few pre-Darwinian species-origin theories Darwin credited at all. Keyserling himself came around to something close to straightforward Darwinism by 1886, five years before his death. One more thread worth pulling, and confirmed directly against Gould's own citation data rather than assumed: Gould did not rely on the 1840 German original alone. A separate, smaller cluster of citations in this Library -- not counted in these 21 -- reads simply "Blas. List of Birds of Eur., Engl. edit.", occasionally spelled out further as "Newton's Eng. edit.": Blasius's own bird chapters from the joint book, later issued in English as A List of the Birds of Europe with the young Alfred Newton, the future Cambridge professor, handling the translation. Gould, in other words, kept both the original German partnership and its later English afterlife on his shelf side by side. One genuine loose thread from the sources themselves, logged rather than resolved: a German biographical dictionary entry for Blasius describes a "joint Russian expedition" with Keyserling in 1840-1841, the same years Blasius's own two-volume travel account, Reise im europaischen Russland in den Jahren 1840 und 1841, was researched. But the more specialized Dictionary of Scientific Biography account of Keyserling's career places him in Russia in 1841 specifically as part of Murchison's survey party, not travelling with Blasius, and credits their only confirmed joint fieldwork to the earlier, pre-1837 Carpathian and Alpine excursions. The two accounts cannot both be describing the same trip without one of them being imprecise, and no source found this pass reconciles them -- so this vignette follows the more specialized, dedicated biography on Keyserling's own 1841 travels and leaves the apparent conflict flagged rather than silently picking a side.

Author & significance There is a small paradox sitting inside a single citation string in Gould's own Library. "G. R. Gray, List of Gen. of Birds" carries 20 citations across 19 species -- a tidy, unremarkable-looking entry, tied for next-most-cited with a Scottish naturalist's manual. But checked line by line against Gould's actual page references, the string turns out to name not one book but two. Ten of the twenty citations point to a slim, single-volume checklist, its pages running from p. 3 to p. 98 and two of them explicitly dated "1840" and "1841" in Gould's own text. The other ten point to something else entirely: a citation reaching into "vol. iii" and page 660, from a three-volume production that would not exist for another three to five years. Both are genuinely by the same author -- Gould simply never bothered to distinguish them, and a century and a half later neither did the algorithm that clustered his citations back into this Library. The man behind both books was George Robert Gray (8 July 1808 -- 6 May 1872), born in Little Chelsea, London, to Samuel Frederick Gray, a working naturalist and pharmacologist, and younger brother of John Edward Gray, who would go on to run the British Museum's entire Zoology department. George joined the Museum himself in 1831, aged twenty-two, as an Assistant in the Zoology Branch -- not, at first, as a bird man at all: his earliest published work was entomological, cataloguing Lepidoptera and, eventually, more than doubling the number of named stick-insect species then known to science, and in 1833 he was a founding member of the Royal Entomological Society of London. Only afterward did he move into ornithology, eventually serving as head of the British Museum's ornithological section for forty-one years. The first of the two books behind this citation, A List of the Genera of Birds, with an Indication of the Typical Species of Each Genus, appeared in 1840 -- a working checklist rather than a narrative, meant to pin down which species stood as the type for each bird genus then recognised. A revised second edition followed in 1841, with a further sixteen-page appendix in 1842; Gould's dated citations, split between "1840" and "1841" and running only as far as p. 98, match this slim single volume exactly. The second, far larger book was The Genera of Birds: Comprising Their Generic Characters, a Notice of the Habits of Each Genus, and an Extensive List of Species Referred to Their Several Genera, issued in three volumes between 1844 and 1849, illustrated by David William Mitchell and, in its later plates, Joseph Wolf, and containing an estimated 46,000 individual references -- one of the most exhaustive single-author indexing projects in nineteenth-century ornithology. Gould's own citation data confirms the identification independently: a handful of entries elsewhere in this Library, kept separate from these twenty, read "Gray and Mitch. Gen. of Birds, vol. iii. p. 653" -- naming Mitchell outright and citing the same volume and page range as the unlabelled half of this cluster. Gray kept at this kind of work for the rest of his career, publishing a further Hand-list of Genera and Species of Birds in three parts between 1869 and 1871, essentially a running catalogue of the British Museum's own collection, before his death in London in 1872. None of it reads as dramatic. It reads as exactly what it was: four decades spent making sure that when a name was attached to a bird, someone could actually go and check it -- work so thorough that even Gould, citing it in a hurry, folded two separate books into one shorthand without anyone noticing for over 170 years.

Author & significance Some say "Sparrm. Mus. Carls" is simply a citation for a set of fine bird plates -- but don't forget Anders Sparrman himself was one of Linnaeus's seventeen "apostles," sent out into the world to fill in the gaps of the Systema Naturae. His path there was about as adventurous as the programme ever produced: after an early trip to China, he joined Captain Cook's second circumnavigation at the Cape of Good Hope, sailed with Cook all the way around the globe on the Resolution, then later pushed into Senegal on an expedition of his own. He was also, by his own account, present at Cook's crew's first sighting of Antarctic ice. He made it home to Uppsala in 1776 and lived to 72 -- Museum Carlsonianum, the plates Gould actually cites, was published in his fifties, the settled harvest of a much wilder younger life.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Apostles of Linnaeus

Author & significance Some say Jacob Theodor Klein was just Linnaeus's failed rival, a man history quietly filed away once the binomial system won -- but don't forget he wasn't trying to build the same thing at all. Klein rejected Linnaeus's system on principle, arguing (with an explicitly biblical justification: that this was how Adam himself must have named the animals) that classification should rest only on immediately visible traits like the number and arrangement of limbs, not on the more abstract internal characters Linnaeus favoured. He was no crank shouting from the sidelines either -- a Fellow of the Royal Society, praised by contemporaries as one of the sharpest natural philosophers of his century, and some of his groupings (particularly among the echinoderms) are still recognisable today. Gould citing "Klein, Av." (his 1750 Historiae Avium Prodromus) isn't a nod to a footnote -- it's a nod to the road not taken, the version of ornithology that might have been if "easily recognisable features" had beaten Linnaeus's more systematic approach.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Jacob Theodor Klein

Verified Barbier Montault. (1838). Notice sur les mœurs du Busard Montagu, Falco cinerascens, Temm. [Notes on the habits of Montagu's Harrier, Falco cinerascens, Temm.]. Revue Zoologique, par la Société Cuvierienne, 1, 121, 222.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
Resolved 2026-08-06 via Jan's own uploaded PDF of this exact 1838 volume (Tome I). The article — "Notice sur les mœurs du Busard Montagu, Falco cinerascens, Temm., par M. Barbier Montault de Loudun" — opens on p. 121 exactly as Gould's citation gives it ('cinerascens, Barb. Rev. Zool. 1838, p. 121'), then continues later in the same volume at p. 222 (a serialized-across-issues article, common for this journal's 'Travaux Inédits' section — not a pagination error). Confirmed independently three ways: pdftotext extraction of the PDF's alphabetical index and running headers, and two of Jan's own high-resolution screenshots of pages 121 and 222 showing the article's opening line and its continuation/footnote naming the author in full: 'M. Barbier Montault, avocat, à Loudun, département de la Vienne' (a lawyer at Loudun, in the Vienne department). Matched to the public archive.org copy (identifier revuezoologiquep38soci) via its metadata: Tome 1, year 1838, Société Cuvierienne, 350 pages — consistent with Jan's PDF (347 pdftotext pages, front matter accounts for the difference). Worth flagging for the record: the species epithet in this article's title is 'Falco cinerascens' (Temminck's usage, exactly as Gould also cites it) — distinct from 'Circus cineraceus' (Montagu, 1802), an earlier and more commonly used synonym for the same bird, Montagu's Harrier. Gould's own citation matches Temminck's spelling precisely, so this is the right match, not a near-miss.
Gould’s own synonymy line names this: cinerascens

Verified Hancock, J. (1839). [Note on Regulus modestus, a British bird]. Annals of Natural History, 2, 310–311.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
"Hane" is a transcription error for Hancock. John Hancock shot Britain's first specimen of Gould's Regulus modestus (later reidentified as the Yellow-browed Warbler) near Hartley, Northumberland, on 26 Sept 1838, and reported it in Annals of Natural History vol. 2. Confirmed two ways: (1) the real archive.org scan of this exact volume (identifier annalsofnaturalh2183unse, London: R. and J.E. Taylor, 1838/39) contains both a "Regulus modestus, Gould, a British Bird" note and a separate article by "J. Hancock, Esq." in the same volume; (2) independently cross-checked against historicalrarebirds.info's account of the first British record, which gives the same name, journal, volume and page (310-311). Have not personally read the exact page-310 text pairing Hancock's name directly to the note, so treat the author attribution as strongly corroborated rather than eyes-on-the-page confirmed.

Vinous Pipit
Author & significance Not yet researched — who this person was and what this reference offered Gould’s readers is a growing feature of this page, filled in a few entries at a time.
Verified Landbeck, C. L. (1843). Die Vögel Sirmiens, ein Beytrag zur Ornithologie und zoologischen Geographie. Isis von Oken, 36, [page range incl. 320].
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
Directly confirmed by searching the real 1843 volume itself (archive.org identifier isisvonoken1843oken, v.36, Isis von Oken, ed. Lorenz Oken) rather than a secondary listing. Page 320 reads: "Die pieperschnäbelige Baumhaubenlerche, Alauda anthirostris mh. (Galerida anthirostris Landb. Syst. Aufzählung S. 36. Nr. 115.)" -- i.e. Landbeck's own "pipit-billed tree-crested lark," naming it Alauda anthirostris mihi (his own coinage) and cross-referencing it to Galerida anthirostris in his own earlier Systematische Aufzählung (p. 36, no. 115, c. 1834-36). This matches Gould's bare citation "Alauda anthirostris, Landb. 1843" exactly on author, year, and name. Note: the paper's actual title is a regional avifauna survey of Sirmien (Syrmia, in the Balkans), not a pipit-specific paper as one early hypothesis suggested -- "pieperschnäbelig" (pipit-billed) is a descriptive term for this particular lark's bill shape, not a sign the paper is about the genus Anthus.

Verified Endler, F. G. (1763-1830), & Scholz, F. P. (1772-1837). (1809-1824). Der Naturfreund, oder Beitraege zur schlesischen Naturgeschichte [The Nature Friend, or Contributions to Silesian Natural History]. C. F. Barth, Breslau.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
RESOLVED, 2026-08-06. Jan located and shared the actual page (Der Naturfreund, p. 17, 'Tab. 5': 'Loxia erytraea, karminrothkoepfige Kernbeisser' / carmine-red-headed grosbeak). Strong match to Gould's citation on two independent points: the plate number ('tab. 5', matching Gould's 'tab. 5, mas' exactly) and the species epithet spelling ('erytraea' vs Gould's 'erythraa'/'erythrea', a period spelling variant). The account, written by the author after finding the bird near Breslau, initially suspects it may be the bird Latham described in 'Syst. Ornith. I p. 445 No. 33'; the specimen was mounted by a Mr. Drescher for Count Mattuschka's natural history collection, later sold to Magdeburg. One point NOT yet reconciled: Gould's citation gives 'tom. i. p. 9' but the located page is numbered 17 in the source's own pagination -- not yet explained, but not doubted given the plate-number and species-name match. The 'et tom. ii. p. 185, tab. 77, fem.' half of Gould's citation (the female specimen, volume 2) has not yet been separately located. Journal confirmed as 'popular science weekly devoted to the flora and fauna of Silesia' (214 plates total, 8vo format, publisher Carl Friedrich Barth, Breslau, 1809-1824), Endler as primary illustrator/creator and Scholz as collaborating illustrator, per the Wroclaw University Library's own digital-collection metadata and a corroborating Muzeum Cyfrowe (Digital Museum) artist-authority record -- both independent of, and used to CORRECT, an initial lead. ACCESS NOTE (2026-08-06): the URL above is a bibliographic metadata/description page on the Wroclaw digital library platform (dLibra) -- it confirms the record but does not itself provide a direct download or page-viewer link; that appears to require a separate interface on the source site not reachable via a simple URL. An alternate link Jan was given (dbc.wroc.pl, edition/131416) was checked directly and returns a 403 error, so it is not given as a direct link. Instead, as a second source to try: Dolnoslaska Biblioteka Cyfrowa (Digital Library of Lower Silesia -- DBC Wroclaw) -- search for 'Der Naturfreund oder Beitraege zur Schlesischen Naturgeschichte' at dbc.wroc.pl (https://www.dbc.wroc.pl).

Verified Picot de Lapeyrouse, P. I. (1782). Beskrifningar och rättelser, hörande til historien om foglarne [Descriptions and corrections pertaining to the history of birds] (C. P. Thunberg, Trans.). Kungliga Svenska Vetenskapsakademiens Handlingar, ser. 2, tom. 3, 104-115+.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
RESOLVED (high confidence), 2026-08-06. Jan located and shared a screenshot of page 104 of this exact journal issue (Apr-Jun 1782), which he found via Biodiversity Heritage Library -- Lapeyrouse's own ornithological submission to the Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences, made the same year he was elected a foreign member of the Academy (1782, independently confirmed via Wikipedia). He then separately shared the full PDF scan (archive.org identifier kungligasvenskav2317kung, confirmed via its own metadata to be 'Series 2, tom. 3' -- an exact match for Gould's 'torn. iii'). Searching the full OCR text of that PDF for Gould's exact species epithet ('audax') found Lapeyrouse's own entry, 'ARDEA audax', at pages 112-113 of this volume -- explicitly tied by Lapeyrouse to 'Le Guacco' (Buffon's Hist. Nat. des Oiseaux, vol. VII p. 390), the old French vernacular name for the Squacco Heron, matching the species Gould's citation is attached to. The distinctive, uncommon epithet 'audax' combined with the correct author, correct journal, correct volume, and an explicit tie to the Squacco Heron's old vernacular name together make this a strong identification. One open point, noted honestly rather than glossed over: Gould's citation gives page 306, but this Swedish-language original has the entry at pages 112-113 -- almost certainly explained by Gould citing a separately-paginated German translation of this same journal ('Neue Schwedische Abhandlungen', the literal German title Gould's abbreviation uses, translated by Kastner and Link, published with its own independent page numbering some years after the Swedish original) rather than this Swedish edition directly -- but the German edition's own copy of this specific article has not yet been located to confirm page 306 directly. Given the strength of the epithet+author+journal+species match, this citation is being marked resolved rather than left in the unidentified list, with this page-number caveat documented for full transparency.

Spoonbill
Author & significance Some say Fredrik Hasselquist's journey through Egypt, Cyprus, Rhodes, Smyrna and the Holy Land was simply a successful Linnaean expedition, its specimens shipped home and its author moving on to the next chapter of a promising career -- but don't forget he never came home at all. Hasselquist died in Smyrna in February 1752, weeks before he'd planned to sail back to Sweden, having also run up debts he had no way to repay. His entire collection -- the specimens Gould's citation ultimately rests on -- was at real risk of being seized and scattered by creditors. It was saved only because Sweden's own queen, Louisa Ulrika, personally paid off the debt so Linnaeus could get the material into print; the resulting book, Iter Palaestinum, appeared five years after its author's death, edited by the teacher he'd never see again.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Apostles of Linnaeus
Verified Hasselquist, F. (1757). Iter Palaestinum, eller Resa til Heliga Landet, förrättad ifrån år 1749 til 1752 (C. Linnaeus, Ed.). Lars Salvius.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
“Falsest” is an OCR mangling of “Palaestinum”. Published posthumously by Linnaeus from Hasselquist's own travel notes; a German translation (“Reise nach Palästina”) also circulated, which Gould cites separately elsewhere (see “Hasselq. Reise”) — same underlying expedition and author, different published edition.

"Simm." is Thomas Williams Simmonds (d. 1804), a Kent physician-naturalist; the paper was read to the Society in 1804 but not published until 1807, matching Gould's raw citation "Simm. in Linn. Trans., vol. viii, p. 264" exactly on volume and page. Confirmed via an independent specialist nomenclature discussion (BirdForum, not the source that first suggested this identification), which quotes the paper's title and gives the same journal/volume/page and names the bird after a Mr. J. Williams of Dartford. Have not personally read the primary page text, so this rests on independent secondary corroboration rather than direct confirmation.

Ross's Gull
Author & significance Some say Johan Peter Falck's survey of the Russian steppes and the Volga region belongs alongside Pallas's in the roll call of Enlightenment scientific triumphs -- and the raw geography he covered does. But don't forget how the story actually ends: worn down by years in the field, Falck developed an opium dependency that deepened into severe depression, and in 1774, in Kazan, he took his own life. His travel journals -- the material behind Gould's "Falk. Reise" citation -- were only assembled and published more than a decade later, in 1785-86, compiled from the notes of a man who never lived to organise them himself. It's a reminder that the Russian Academy's expeditions of this era weren't a uniformly heroic story -- Falck's name sits on the same list as Pallas's, but his path through it was a much harder one.
Checked against: Wikipedia: Apostles of Linnaeus

Corrected per Jan's suggestion: Gould's abbreviation 'Gen. Rep.' matches this work's actual title far more precisely than the similarly-themed Vol. 9 'Birds' report I'd first cited -- these Pacific Railroad Survey volumes have a genuinely messy publication history, reprinted across several congressional sessions with volume numbers shifting between printings (1857, 1858, and 1859 editions all exist for related material), but an antiquarian bookseller record specifically dates this 'General Report' (Vol. 8) to 1857, matching the date Jan proposed. The archive.org link above is to the companion Vol. 9 Birds report, kept as the most reliable freely-accessible scan pending a confirmed link for Vol. 8 itself.

Verified Gray, G. R. (1849). The genera of birds: Comprising their generic characters, a notice of the habits of each genus, and an extensive list of species referred to their several genera (Vol. 3). Longman, Brown, Green, and Longmans.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
Illustrated by David William Mitchell (hence 'Gray and Mitch.'). Published in parts 1844–1849; volume 3, which contains the Stercorarius (skua) entry Gould cites, was completed in 1849.
Gould’s own synonymy line names this: Stercorarius pomarinus

Verified Swainson, W., & Richardson, J. (1831). Fauna Boreali-Americana; or, the Zoology of the Northern Parts of British America ... Part Second: The Birds. John Murray.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
Same work as the already-verified 'Swains. and Rich. Faun. Bor.-Am. Birds' entry. The leading 'Leach,' is likely an OCR merge with an adjacent, unrelated citation fragment (William Elford Leach was a contemporary naturalist, but is not an author of this particular work).

Verified Strickland, H. E. (1830-1832). [Contributions]. In Proceedings of the Committee of Science and Correspondence of the Zoological Society of London.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
Hugh Edwin Strickland was a regular contributor to this early Zoological Society journal; the specific individual article/volume Gould intended could not be pinned down further, so this cites the journal itself via its BHL bibliography record.

Author & significance Not yet researched — who this person was and what this reference offered Gould’s readers is a growing feature of this page, filled in a few entries at a time.
Verified Reichenbach, H. G. L. (1850). Avium systema naturale: Das natürliche System der Vögel mit hundert Tafeln grösstentheils Original-Abbildungen.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
Jan independently identified this as Reichenbach rather than Wagler's differently-titled 1827 'Systema avium' (which an earlier round of mine had mistakenly flagged as a look-alike and set aside) -- confirmed via BHL, which holds Reichenbach's actual synoptic bird survey under this title.
Gould’s own synonymy line names this: Ardenna major
Great Shearwater
Author & significance Not yet researched — who this person was and what this reference offered Gould’s readers is a growing feature of this page, filled in a few entries at a time.
Verified Faber, F. (1822). Prodromus der isländischen Ornithologie oder Geschichte der Vögel Islands. Riöpping.
Cited 1 time across 1 species · View source & download →
'Fab.' here is Frederik Faber (not Fabricius) — his 1822 Prodromus der isländischen Ornithologie was the standard early-19th-century reference on Icelandic birds, and is cited as such elsewhere in Gould's text.
